<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove a sequential elliptic inequality for the renormalized Einstein–Hilbert functional on a fixed finite tree of four-dimensional Ricci-flat spaces. As the joining lengths diverge and the scale-neutral weighted Ricci error <i>M</i> tends to zero, the functional satisfies $`E=o(M)`$. The root end has decay exponent greater than one, and every joined quotient group is nontrivial. We also prove the multivariable path-selection theorem for asymptotic expansions used to obtain this estimate.

- **claim-first-variation-scaling** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-functional)): The functional in Equation <a href="#eq:energy-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-functional">[eq:energy-functional]</a> is defined on the preceding metrics. For a variation with the same root decay, $$DE_g(\dot g)=\int\left\langle\tfrac12R(g)g-\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(g),\dot g\right\rangle_g \,d\mu_g. \label{ell:first-variation}$$ It is invariant under diffeomorphisms isotopic to the identity whose root displacement, including scaled derivatives throughout the isotopy, is $O(r^{1-q'})$ for some $q'>1$. A global length scaling by $s$, accompanied by a coordinate dilation at infinity restoring the leading tensor $\delta$, multiplies $E$ by $s^2$. Under Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a>, $\|E(g)\|\le CM$.
  - Explanation: The renormalized functional is well defined on the admissible metrics and its first variation is the pairing with the Einstein tensor. It is invariant under root-decaying isotopic diffeomorphisms, scales homogeneously under global length scaling, and is controlled linearly by the weighted Ricci error.
- **claim-tree-inequality** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-tree-inequality)): Fix a finite rooted tree with the end identifications and order-$20$ metric bounds of Section <a href="#ell:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:setup">2.1</a>. Let $(g_j,L^{(j)},M_j)$ satisfy the compact vertex convergence, the same uniform half-end and root-end bounds, and Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a> through order $10$, with every $L_e^{(j)}\to\infty$ and $M_j\to0$. Then, in physical root units, $$E(g_j)=o(M_j). \label{ell:little-oh}$$ For an index with $M_j=0$, the assertion means $E(g_j)=0$. The little-oh is taken for this fixed tree; it does not assert a power exponent uniform over different trees.
  - Explanation: Along sequences on a fixed finite tree with all joining lengths diverging and the weighted Ricci error tending to zero, the renormalized functional is of smaller order than the error. The statement is sequential for one tree and gives no exponent uniform over trees.
- **claim-path-selection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-path-selection)): Let $P_1,\ldots,P_s$ be an admissible finite family, and let a finite Boolean combination of conditions $P_j>0$, $P_j=0$, and $P_j<0$ hold along a sequence $$L_i^{(n)}\longrightarrow+\infty,\qquad z^{(n)}\longrightarrow z_0.$$ There is a real analytic map $u\mapsto(L(u),z(u))$, defined for all sufficiently large real $u$, with the same limits and satisfying those conditions on its tail. Each actual coordinate $L_i(u)$ and $z_a(u)$ is eventually monotone or constant. Given any additional finite admissible list, its values on the path can also be required to be eventually monotone or constant. Finite prescribed limits of functions can be imposed at the same time.
  - Explanation: Finite sign conditions and finite prescribed limits holding along a divergent sequence for an admissible family can be realized along a real analytic path whose actual coordinates are eventually monotone or constant.
- **claim-variation-consequence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-variation)): Suppose admissible functions $F,J$, with $J\ge0$, satisfy $$\|dF\|\le C\sqrt J\left(\sum_a\|dz_a\| +\sum_i e^{-cL_i}\|dL_i\|\right),\qquad c>0,$$ on real parameter domains. There cannot be a sequence with $L_i\to\infty$, an interior ordinary-parameter limit, and $F\ne0$, $F^2\ge c_0J$, $F\to0$, where $c_0>0$.
  - Explanation: A gradient bound of the admissible function by the square root of a nonnegative admissible function rules out sequences where the function tends to zero while staying comparable to that square root. The argument shows finite variation of the logarithm of the function.
- **claim-sector-uniqueness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-sector-uniqueness)): Suppose $f$ is bounded and holomorphic on an admissible sector and, on the central subsector $\|\arg z\|\le\pi/4$, satisfies $f(z)=O_B(e^{-B\|z\|})$ for every $B>0$. Then $f=0$.
  - Explanation: A bounded holomorphic function on an admissible sector that decays faster than any exponential on the central subsector must vanish identically.
- **claim-clock-calculus** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-clock)): The preceding construction has the following properties, established simultaneously by induction.* 1. *Each $B_j$ is a field, closed under differentiation in $x_1$ and conjugation. A nonzero scalar has a leading equivalent $$\label{path:eq-leading-monomial} Cx_1^p\exp\!\left(\sum_{k=1}^j\beta_kx_k\right), \qquad C\ne0,$$ uniformly on a suitable admissible sector. Its real subfield is a Hardy field: every nonzero real germ has a fixed eventual sign, and its derivative either vanishes or has a fixed eventual sign.* 2. *If $H\in B_j$ is positive and $x_j=o(H)$, it is a valid new sector variable. Finitely many prescribed earlier-clock sectors can be reached by taking a sufficiently narrow admissible $H$-sector. If $f\in B_j$ is positive, unbounded, and $f=O(H)$, then on such a sector its modulus is comparable to its value at the corresponding positive real $H=\|H\|$; it maps into any finitely prescribed admissible $f$-sectors. If $f=o(H)$ on the real ray, then $\Re f=o(\Re H)$ throughout the narrowed sector.
  - Explanation: The tower of clock fields is closed under field operations, differentiation and conjugation, has explicit leading monomials, and its real part is a Hardy field. A change of clock to a faster positive germ is valid on suitably narrowed sectors.
- **claim-sectorial-family-limits** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-family-limits)): Let $F$ be a holomorphic family in a finite clock hierarchy, on an ordinary polydisc $D$. Suppose that on the positive path ray it converges to a finite holomorphic family $F_\infty$, locally uniformly on $D$. Then on every fixed smaller polydisc $D'\Subset D$, it converges uniformly to the same family on some admissible sector of its last clock. The conclusion remains valid after passing to a faster admissible clock by Lemma <a href="#path:clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:clock">8</a>. Finitely many such requirements can be imposed on one sector. In particular, a normalized limit which is nonzero on a fixed compact contour stays uniformly nonzero there, and finitely many composition arguments with limiting images in prescribed open domains stay in those domains.
  - Explanation: A holomorphic family that converges along the positive path ray converges uniformly on a sector of its last clock, on smaller polydiscs, and finitely many such requirements can be imposed together.
- **claim-prepared-contours** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-prepared-contours)): Let $F(q,\zeta,y)$ belong to a regular system. Suppose that after scalar normalization its limiting germ is regular of degree $d$ in the $y$-direction at $(\zeta,y)=(0,0)$. Then its Weierstrass polynomial, its unit, and division of any other family by that polynomial are obtained by the operations in <span class="upright">(R3)</span> on fixed contours and fixed smaller parameter polydiscs. Arbitrarily high Taylor coefficients of these operations use the same denominator zero sets and the same contours.
  - Explanation: Weierstrass preparation and division in a regular system are obtained from fixed-contour operations, and all higher Taylor coefficients reuse the same denominators and contours.
- **claim-regular-roots** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-roots)): Suppose <span class="upright">(R1)–(R3)</span> hold, including divisibility of positive sizes. Then the complex scalar field is algebraically closed and its real part is real closed.
  - Explanation: A regular scalar system with divisibility of positive sizes has an algebraically closed complex scalar field and a real closed real part, shown by splitting monic polynomials via contour power sums.
- **claim-detection-of-germs** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-detection)): Path families with the submersivity property in <span class="upright">(R4)</span> detect analytic germs. Namely, if an analytic function vanishes on the tail of every path in such a family, then it vanishes as a domain germ. The same holds for a holomorphic family in extra ordinary variables.
  - Explanation: Submersive path families detect analytic germs: vanishing on the tail of every path forces vanishing as a domain germ, because zero sets of nonzero real analytic functions are null.
- **claim-adjoin-reciprocal** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-adjoin-p)): Let $\mathcal A$ be a regular system for a nonempty lower tuple $L'=(L'_1,\ldots,L'_r)$, with real scalar field $K_{\mathbb{R}}$. Assume that each $L'_i$ is a positive unbounded element of $K_{\mathbb{R}}$, and put $M'=1+\sum_{i=1}^rL'_i$. Assume in addition that, for every finite use of its path property, the lower paths can be chosen with last clock $x_{\rm prev}=O(M')$. This is an additional property of the length systems constructed below; it is not part of the abstract conditions <span class="upright">(R1)–(R4)</span>. Let a new independent positive variable $p$ have the distinguished limits $$p\longrightarrow0,\qquad p\max L'\longrightarrow0, \qquad \|p-c\|\gtrsim p^2\quad(c\in K_{\mathbb{R}}).$$ The constant in the last comparison may depend on $c$. Use domain fibers in $p$ which are open intervals cut out by finitely many strict comparisons with lower scalars. There is a regular system $\mathcal A[p]$ containing the lower system and $p,p^{-1}$, closed under the finite analytic operations, with the following size bound for each nonzero scalar and its reciprocal: $$\label{path:eq-p-size} \|a\|+\|a\|^{-1}\le e^{C(1+\max L')}p^{-C}.$$ Its finite path requirements can be realized by substituting for $p$ an expression in lower scalars, with an additional real constant varying in an open interval, and preserving $pM'\to0$. On the resulting lower paths one therefore has $px_{\rm prev}\to0$.* *For the empty lower tuple the same conclusion holds with ordinary holomorphic germs and the choice $p=\theta/x_1$, $\theta>0$.
  - Explanation: A regular system can be extended by an independent reciprocal fastest length under a quantitative separation hypothesis, with a size bound on scalars and reciprocals and with path families preserved.
- **claim-centers-regular-systems** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-centers)): For a distinguished sequence of $m$ lengths tending to infinity, there are centers $L_1^*,\ldots,L_m^*$, differing from the sequence values by bounded additive amounts, and a regular system on a domain base with the following additional properties.* 1. *The system contains the centers, $e^{bL_i^*}$ for every real $b$, and every admissible family evaluated at these centers.* 2. *Every finite expression built with the allowed analytic operations has coefficient formulas on a common connected domain of the base and a common ordinary-parameter neighborhood. The same formulas remain valid to all orders on paths preserving its finite preparation data. Remainder sectors and thresholds may depend on order.* 3. *On the path families, the grouped centers have the form $c_iX+D_i^*$, $c_i>0$, where the positive magnitudes of the unbounded $D_i^*$ are slower centers. All required quadratic holomorphy domains are reached after narrowing the clock sectors.* 4. *For a nonempty tuple, its path families can be chosen so that the last clock is the selected fastest center $X$. Every center is $O(X)$, and one of the centers equals $X$. In particular the last clock is comparable to $\max_iL_i^*$, independently of which finite expression requirements are imposed.
  - Explanation: For several diverging lengths there are centers within bounded distance of the sequence and a regular system on a domain base closed under finite analytic operations, with the last clock comparable to the largest center.
- **claim-sign-elimination** (theorem, [source](#stmt-path-sign-elimination)): For a finite list of real polynomials $P_i(y)$ of bounded degrees, existence of a real $y$ realizing any specified Boolean combination of their signs is determined by finitely many polynomial sign tests in their coefficients. The same tests are valid for polynomial coefficients that are real scalar germs in a regular system.
  - Explanation: Existence of a real point with prescribed sign pattern for finitely many bounded-degree polynomials is decided by finitely many polynomial sign tests in the coefficients, and the same tests hold over regular scalar germs.
- **claim-disconnected-inverse** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-disconnected-inverse)): There are finitely many fixed smooth core-supported sources and finitely many fixed linear tests on core compact sets such that each disconnected operator, augmented by these sources and tests, is an isomorphism $X^k_\gamma\oplus\mathbb{R}^a\to Y^{k-2}_\gamma\oplus\mathbb{R}^b$. The same choices work for the selected orders and nearby sufficiently small positive joining weights. They work for $b_H$, with uniformly bounded inverse, when $H$ is sufficiently large.
  - Explanation: Finite core sources and core tests make each disconnected linearized operator an isomorphism with uniformly bounded inverse, for nearby weights and large truncation.
- **claim-seam-inverse** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-seam-inverse)): Adjoin these seam data to the disconnected augmentations. For all sufficiently large real lengths the resulting piecewise problem is an isomorphism, uniformly in the lengths. Its data norms at an edge are $e^{\gamma L_e}H^{k-1/2}$ for value jumps and critical tests and $e^{\gamma L_e}H^{k-3/2}$ for derivative jumps. The assertion includes any partial disconnection of the tree, with the tests and matching omitted on opened edges.
  - Explanation: Adding seam matching data keeps the augmented problem an isomorphism uniformly in the lengths, including every partial disconnection of the tree.
- **claim-complex-inverse** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-complex-inverse)): For some $A,c_0>0$ the inverse of Lemma <a href="#ell:seam-inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:seam-inverse">18</a> continues to $$\Re L_e>A,\qquad \|\Im L_e\|<c_0(\Re L_e)^2. \label{ell:quadratic-domain}$$ The field norm is measured on increasing-real-part contours. One strengthens the source norm on the portion $u\ge\Re L_e/5$ and the seam norm by a fixed polynomial in $1+\Re L_e$. With these norms the inverse is uniform. The same assertion holds for opened ends continued along such contours and for every partial disconnection.
  - Explanation: The uniform seam inverse continues to complex lengths in quadratic regions, using strengthened source and seam norms measured along bent contours.
- **claim-gauge** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-gauge)): After a diffeomorphism of the invariance class in Lemma <a href="#ell:functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:functional">1</a>, each sufficiently late metric in the sequence satisfies $$W_b(g)=m\cdot\psi, \label{ell:gauge-equation}$$ where $\psi$ is the finite list of vector augmentation sources, rescaled in physical units, and $m$ is small. The gauged metric retains Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a> in weaker admissible weights and is close to $b$ at the joining weight $\gamma_g$.
  - Explanation: After an admissible diffeomorphism each sufficiently late metric satisfies a gauge condition with a small coefficient vector, retains the Ricci bound in weaker weights and stays close to the background.
- **claim-analytic-small-branch** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-analytic-family)): On a fixed complex polydisc in $z$ and the quadratic length domain of Equation <a href="#ell:quadratic-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:quadratic-domain">[ell:quadratic-domain]</a>, Equation <a href="#ell:family-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:family-equation">[ell:family-equation]</a> has a unique small solution $(U,d)$. It obeys uniform bounds at both joining weights $\nu$ and $\nu'$. Its augmented linearization has the same uniform inverse bounds. These assertions hold for every partial disconnection on common smaller domains. The family, its compact observables, and its endpoint values are single-valued holomorphic functions of the length parameters.
  - Explanation: The augmented family equation has a unique small solution on a fixed polydisc and the quadratic length domain, with uniform bounds and single-valued holomorphic dependence on the lengths.
- **claim-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-comparison)): Choose $z$ from the core tests and gauge coefficients of $g$, and from Equation <a href="#ell:seam-prescription" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:seam-prescription">[ell:seam-prescription]</a> at its seams. Then $$\\|g-h_*\\|_{X^k_\nu}\le CM,\qquad \\|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(h_*)\\|_{Y^{k-2}_\nu}\le CM,\qquad \|E(g)-E(h_*)\|\le CM^2. \label{ell:comparison-estimates}$$
  - Explanation: The gauged actual metric is within a multiple of the Ricci error of the analytic family, the family has Ricci of that size, and the functional values differ by the square of that size.
- **claim-flat-tail-improvement** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-flat-tail)): Let $\mathcal P$ be either flat operator above. Suppose a field has a finite growth bound and its source has an expansion to rate $B$, where the intermediate remainder weights avoid the indicial roots. Then the field has an expansion to that rate, with free homogeneous coefficients recorded at the crossed roots. Estimates involve the source coefficients and remainder, a fixed inner trace, and the previous growth bound. They are uniform on the contours of Lemma <a href="#ell:complex-inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:complex-inverse">19</a>, with an arbitrarily small loss in a strictly available exponential rate.
  - Explanation: For the flat componentwise operators, a field with finite growth and a source expansion to a given rate has an expansion to that rate, with free homogeneous coefficients recorded at crossed indicial roots.
- **claim-prescribed-tails** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-prescribed-tails)): The small solution of Lemma <a href="#ell:analytic-family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:analytic-family">21</a> on every partial disconnection has integer tails with only $l>0$. Its augmented linearization, denoted $\mathcal A_U$, can be solved for a source with a finite-growth integer tail, with arbitrary finitely supported prescriptions of free homogeneous coefficients in the nondecaying indicial channels. Unspecified coefficients are zero, so the prescribed growth has a finite maximum. The result has an integer tail; its growth does not exceed the maximum of zero, the forced growth, and the prescribed growth, apart from polynomial factors. The solution is unique with these prescriptions and the other augmented data. Estimates to any fixed finite precision are linear in the data, with constants allowed to depend on that precision and growth. Every finite tail coefficient depends analytically on the parameters on one common domain.
  - Explanation: The small nonlinear solution has purely decaying integer tails, and the augmented linearization can be solved uniquely for sources with finite-growth integer tails and prescribed free coefficients, analytically on one common domain.
- **claim-triangular-systems** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-triangular)): Lemma <a href="#ell:prescribed-tails" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:prescribed-tails">25</a> applies successively to every finite system appended to the small solution in which each equation has diagonal operator $\mathcal A_U$ and source given by Taylor differential operations on earlier fields. Allow finite growth at already opened ends, and free asymptotic conditions depending polynomially on finitely many earlier tail coefficients. On still-finite seams impose homogeneous matching and zero added critical tests. Require that sources on those halves have at least two decaying factors, counting the base field and its derivatives. On opening a new pair of ends, start with decay there. The resulting fields and the inverse of the triangular linearization have uniform finite-precision bounds and the same analytic parameter domain.
  - Explanation: The tail solvability extends successively to finite triangular systems whose diagonal operator is the augmented linearization, with uniform finite-precision bounds on the same parameter domain.
- **claim-one-length-expansion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-one-length)): For every desired exponential precision, the actual small solution and every appended field have an expansion by finitely many terms in Equation <a href="#ell:length-terms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:length-terms">[ell:length-terms]</a>, with an exponentially better remainder. It is uniform in the remaining finite lengths on their quadratic regions, in a fixed smaller ordinary-parameter polydisc. For older opened ends the conclusion holds in every specified finite tail norm. Coefficient fields are obtained by the finite constructions in Lemmas <a href="#ell:prescribed-tails" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:prescribed-tails">25</a> and <a href="#ell:triangular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:triangular">26</a>.
  - Explanation: The small solution and appended fields have finite expansions in one length to any exponential precision, with exponentially better remainders, uniform in the other lengths and in a fixed ordinary-parameter polydisc.
- **claim-finite-part-integration** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-finite-parts)): Integration of the coefficient fields constructed in Lemma <a href="#ell:one-length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:one-length">27</a> preserves exponential-polynomial length expansions for $F$ and $J$. Each resulting coefficient is a finite sum of core or root-tail integrals, finite-part integrals on opened ends, and evaluations of finite tail coefficients, on the corresponding partial disconnection. These operations are analytic and continuous in sufficiently high finite tail precision.
  - Explanation: Integrating the coefficient fields preserves exponential-polynomial length expansions of the functional and the squared Ricci observable, via finite-part integrals and tail evaluations.
- **claim-all-order-expansion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-all-orders)): The functions $F,J$ satisfy the analytic expansion hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:path-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:path-selection">4</a>. Their length exponents belong to the discrete nonnegative grid $\lambda\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}$. In any length, and recursively after taking any finite list of coefficients, expansions have exponentially better remainders at every cutoff. All recursively obtained coefficients are analytic on a common fixed-width complex tail band of the remaining lengths and one common open complex neighborhood of the ordinary parameters. The neighborhood does not decrease with the expansion order.
  - Explanation: The functional and the squared Ricci observable meet the analytic expansion hypotheses of the path-selection theorem, with exponents on a discrete grid and a common analyticity domain that does not shrink with the order.
- **claim-differential-estimate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ell-differential)): On the real small family, for some $c>0$, $$\|dF\|\le C\sqrt J\left(\sum_\alpha\|dz_\alpha\| +\sum_e e^{-cL_e}\|dL_e\|\right). \label{ell:dF}$$
  - Explanation: On the real small family the differential of the functional is bounded by the square root of the Ricci observable times the weighted variation of ordinary parameters and lengths.

Finitely many such requirements can be imposed on one sector. In particular, a normalized limit which is nonzero on a fixed compact contour stays uniformly nonzero there, and finitely many composition arguments with limiting images in prescribed open domains stay in those domains.",
          "source_id": "PM-351.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $F$ be a holomorphic family in a finite clock hierarchy, on an ordinary polydisc $D$. Suppose that on the positive path ray it converges to a finite holomorphic family $F_\\infty$, locally uniformly on $D$. Then on every fixed smaller polydisc $D'\\Subset D$, it converges uniformly to the same family on some admissible sector of its last clock. The conclusion remains valid after passing to a faster admissible clock by Lemma <a href=\"#path:clock\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"path:clock\">8</a>. Finitely many such requirements can be imposed on one sector. In particular, a normalized limit which is nonzero on a fixed compact contour stays uniformly nonzero there, and finitely many composition arguments with limiting images in prescribed open domains stay in those domains.",

        "anchor": "stmt-path-adjoin-p",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal A$ be a regular system for a nonempty lower tuple $L'=(L'_1,\\ldots,L'_r)$, with real scalar field $K_{\\mathbb{R}}$. Assume that each $L'_i$ is a positive unbounded element of $K_{\\mathbb{R}}$, and put $M'=1+\\sum_{i=1}^rL'_i$. Assume in addition that, for every finite use of its path property, the lower paths can be chosen with last clock $x_{\\rm prev}=O(M')$. This is an additional property of the length systems constructed below; it is not part of the abstract conditions <span class=\"upright\">(R1)–(R4)</span>. Let a new independent positive variable $p$ have the distinguished limits $$p\\longrightarrow0,\\qquad p\\max L'\\longrightarrow0,\n \\qquad |p-c|\\gtrsim p^2\\quad(c\\in K_{\\mathbb{R}}).$$ The constant in the last comparison may depend on $c$. Use domain fibers in $p$ which are open intervals cut out by finitely many strict comparisons with lower scalars. There is a regular system $\\mathcal A[p]$ containing the lower system and $p,p^{-1}$, closed under the finite analytic operations, with the following size bound for each nonzero scalar and its reciprocal: $$\\label{path:eq-p-size}\n |a|+|a|^{-1}\\le e^{C(1+\\max L')}p^{-C}.$$ Its finite path requirements can be realized by substituting for $p$ an expression in lower scalars, with an additional real constant varying in an open interval, and preserving $pM'\\to0$. On the resulting lower paths one therefore has $px_{\\rm prev}\\to0$.*\n\n*For the empty lower tuple the same conclusion holds with ordinary holomorphic germs and the choice $p=\\theta/x_1$, $\\theta>0$.",

        "anchor": "stmt-path-centers",
        "exact_quote": "For a distinguished sequence of $m$ lengths tending to infinity, there are centers $L_1^*,\\ldots,L_m^*$, differing from the sequence values by bounded additive amounts, and a regular system on a domain base with the following additional properties.*\n\n1.  *The system contains the centers, $e^{bL_i^*}$ for every real $b$, and every admissible family evaluated at these centers.*\n\n2.  *Every finite expression built with the allowed analytic operations has coefficient formulas on a common connected domain of the base and a common ordinary-parameter neighborhood. The same formulas remain valid to all orders on paths preserving its finite preparation data. Remainder sectors and thresholds may depend on order.*\n\n3.  *On the path families, the grouped centers have the form $c_iX+D_i^*$, $c_i>0$, where the positive magnitudes of the unbounded $D_i^*$ are slower centers. All required quadratic holomorphy domains are reached after narrowing the clock sectors.*\n\n4.  *For a nonempty tuple, its path families can be chosen so that the last clock is the selected fastest center $X$. Every center is $O(X)$, and one of the centers equals $X$. In particular the last clock is comparable to $\\max_iL_i^*$, independently of which finite expression requirements are imposed.",

        "anchor": "stmt-ell-gauge",
        "exact_quote": "After a diffeomorphism of the invariance class in Lemma <a href=\"#ell:functional\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ell:functional\">1</a>, each sufficiently late metric in the sequence satisfies $$W_b(g)=m\\cdot\\psi,\n \\label{ell:gauge-equation}$$ where $\\psi$ is the finite list of vector augmentation sources, rescaled in physical units, and $m$ is small.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ell-flat-tail",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal P$ be either flat operator above. Suppose a field has a finite growth bound and its source has an expansion to rate $B$, where the intermediate remainder weights avoid the indicial roots. Then the field has an expansion to that rate, with free homogeneous coefficients recorded at the crossed roots. Estimates involve the source coefficients and remainder, a fixed inner trace, and the previous growth bound. They are uniform on the contours of Lemma <a href=\"#ell:complex-inverse\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ell:complex-inverse\">19</a>, with an arbitrarily small loss in a strictly available exponential rate.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ell-prescribed-tails",
        "exact_quote": "The small solution of Lemma <a href=\"#ell:analytic-family\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ell:analytic-family\">21</a> on every partial disconnection has integer tails with only $l>0$. Its augmented linearization, denoted $\\mathcal A_U$, can be solved for a source with a finite-growth integer tail, with arbitrary finitely supported prescriptions of free homogeneous coefficients in the nondecaying indicial channels. Unspecified coefficients are zero, so the prescribed growth has a finite maximum. The result has an integer tail; its growth does not exceed the maximum of zero, the forced growth, and the prescribed growth, apart from polynomial factors. The solution is unique with these prescriptions and the other augmented data. Estimates to any fixed finite precision are linear in the data, with constants allowed to depend on that precision and growth. Every finite tail coefficient depends analytically on the parameters on one common domain.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ell-triangular",
        "exact_quote": "Lemma <a href=\"#ell:prescribed-tails\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ell:prescribed-tails\">25</a> applies successively to every finite system appended to the small solution in which each equation has diagonal operator $\\mathcal A_U$ and source given by Taylor differential operations on earlier fields. Allow finite growth at already opened ends, and free asymptotic conditions depending polynomially on finitely many earlier tail coefficients. On still-finite seams impose homogeneous matching and zero added critical tests. Require that sources on those halves have at least two decaying factors, counting the base field and its derivatives. On opening a new pair of ends, start with decay there. The resulting fields and the inverse of the triangular linearization have uniform finite-precision bounds and the same analytic parameter domain.",

The neighborhood does not decrease with the expansion order.",
          "source_id": "PM-351.3"
        }

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **admissible family** ([source](#stmt-path-def-admissible)): An admissible family in $m$ lengths consists of real holomorphic functions $P(L_1,\ldots,L_m,z)$, with the following properties. These properties are required for every recursively extracted coefficient as well as for the initial functions. 1. The functions are bounded on a product of regions $Q(A,c)$, locally uniformly in the ordinary parameters. 2. For each length there is an expansion $$\label{path:eq-expansion} P(L,z)\sim\sum_{d\in D_i}e^{-dL_i} \sum_{k=0}^{k_d}L_i^k P_{dk}(L_{\widehat i},z).$$ Here $D_i\subset[0,\infty)$ is locally finite, each $k_d$ is finite, and $k_0=0$ if $0\in D_i$. For every cutoff $B$, subtracting the terms with $d\le B$ leaves a remainder bounded by $C_Be^{-(B+\epsilon_B)\Re L_i}$, for some $\epsilon_B>0$, on possibly smaller quadratic regions. Bounds are uniform on each fixed smaller ordinary-parameter polydisc. 3. For every subset of expanded lengths, all its coefficient formulas, at all orders, are holomorphic on one common tail band $$\prod_{j\text{ unexpanded}} \{\Re L_j>A',\ \|\Im L_j\|<c'\},\qquad c'>0,$$ times one common open ordinary-parameter neighborhood. These domains may be shrunk once when a finite number of families are used together. The constants and thresholds in asymptotic *estimates* may depend on the order; the common domains of *analyticity* do not. 4. If a subset of real parts has the form $\Re L_i=c_iU+o(U)$, where $c_i>0$, the corresponding iterated expansion can be grouped by weights $\sum_i c_id_i$. The grouped weights are locally finite, and each finite cutoff has a remainder of strictly better exponential order in $U$. The assertion holds on the complex neighborhoods in <span class="upright">(i)</span> and locally uniformly on the common ordinary-parameter neighborhood. It is enough to establish it by successive single-length expansions. When $m=0$, admissible families are ordinary holomorphic germs.
- **admissible angular loss** ([source](#stmt-path-def-sector)): An admissible angular loss is a positive continuous function $\omega(r)$, for large $r$, such that $$\omega(r)\ge(\log r)^{-2},\qquad \sum_{n\ge n_0}\sup_{2^n<r<2^{n+2}}\omega(r)<\infty.$$ Its sector is the open set $S_\omega=\{z:\|z\|>R,\ \|\arg z\|<\pi/2-\omega(\|z\|)\}$. Every positive loss satisfying the displayed bounds has a continuous admissible enlargement: at dyadic radii choose a summable majorant of the neighboring shell suprema and interpolate linearly in $\log r$. Thus enlarging a loss to make its sector open only shrinks that sector. Increasing a loss by a fixed factor or taking a continuous majorant of the suprema over finitely many neighboring dyadic shells is permitted. Given finitely many losses we can dominate all of them by another admissible loss. We can also arrange that the domination ratio tends to infinity: if $a_n\ge0$ is summable, choose increasing constants $c_n\to\infty$, sufficiently slowly that $\sum c_na_n<\infty$.
- **domain base and regular system** ([source](#stmt-path-def-regular)): A domain base is a directed family of connected real open domains in auxiliary independent variables, each containing the distinguished sequence for a set of indices in $\mathcal U$. A germ on the base is an analytic function on one such domain, with its local complexification; two functions are identified if they agree on a smaller domain. Extra ordinary variables range over complex polydiscs about specified standard values. A regular system $\mathcal A$, with scalar field $K$, has the following properties. 1. Every nonzero family $F(q,\zeta)$ admits a nonzero scalar $k\in K$ such that $F/k$ has a finite, nonzero holomorphic limit as a germ in $\zeta$, locally uniformly on a polydisc, along the domain base. The scalar reciprocal $1/k$ is in the system. For real families $k$ can be chosen real. Every scalar comparison or finite scalar limit made at the distinguished sequence holds along the base after the appropriate restrictions. 2. For every positive scalar size $a$ and integer $n>0$, there is a positive scalar $\rho$ with $\rho^n/a$ tending to a finite positive constant. Scalars and reciprocals have magnitude at most $\exp(C\max_i L_i^*)$, where $L_i^*$ are the current centers. For an empty tuple the bound is constant. 3. The system is closed under finite sums and products, reflection, ordinary holomorphic composition at finite limiting arguments, inversion after a nonvanishing normalized limit has been specified, and Cauchy integration on fixed compact contours. Integrals over fixed compact segments are allowed by a finite cover of their holomorphic parameter neighborhoods. Domain restrictions for these operations are part of the finite data of the expression. 4. Given a domain and finitely many normalization, comparison, and limit requirements, there are families of real analytic paths eventually in that domain preserving them. The expressions used belong on each path to a clock hierarchy of Lemma <a href="#path:clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:clock">8</a>, with parameter versions for any finite specified uses. The path families have real constant parameters in open sets and are analytic in those constants where defined. At every sufficiently large fixed path time, their evaluation into the independent-variable domain is locally submersive. The starting time may depend on the constants. Limits of families in <span class="upright">(R1)</span> mean uniform limits on each fixed smaller polydisc. A nonzero holomorphic limit need not be nonzero at its central point. An inverse requires nonvanishing on the particular polydisc or contour where that inverse is used.
- **integer tail expansion** ([source](#stmt-ell-tail-definition)): On an opened joined-type end, a field has an integer tail if, for each nonintegral rate $B$, it can be written $$Y(\tau,\theta)= \sum_{l<B}e^{-l\tau}\sum_{j=0}^{d_l}\tau^jY_{lj}(\theta) +Y_{>B}. \label{ell:tail-series}$$ Here $l\in\mathbb{Z}$. There is a lower bound for $l$, each $Y_{lj}$ is a finite equivariant spherical harmonic sum, and the sum is finite. The remainder belongs to the cylindrical Sobolev space with any fixed weight strictly below $B$. A bound to finite precision controls the finitely many displayed coefficients and that remainder norm. Polynomial contour costs can be paid by a strictly smaller remainder rate. For a growing field this is a bound after subtraction of the finite nondecaying tail; it is not a bound on the unsubtracted field at infinity.

| q-ricci-observable-bound | squared Ricci observable J on the approximants | ≤ | $CM^2$ | real family |
| q-main-little-oh | renormalized functional along the sequence | = | $o(M_j)$ | sequential statement for one fixed tree |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-first-variation** [claim](#stmt-ell-functional) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-functional): Convergence of the scalar-curvature density, integration by parts, Bianchi identity, differentiation of the scaling law. -> first variation, invariance, scaling and linear bound on the functional
- **step-admissible-class** [claim](#stmt-path-def-admissible) / [proof](#stmt-path-def-admissible): Definition of the class of functions to which path selection applies. -> admissible families
- **step-sector-uniqueness** [claim](#stmt-path-sector-uniqueness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-sector-uniqueness): Conformal pullback to a half-plane, harmonic measure and subharmonic maximum principle, identity theorem. -> uniqueness of superexponentially small germs
- **step-clock-calculus** [claim](#stmt-path-clock) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-clock): Simultaneous induction over the clock hierarchy with Cauchy estimates and analytic inversion. -> Hardy field of clock germs and change of clock
- **step-family-limits** [claim](#stmt-path-family-limits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-family-limits): Induction on the hierarchy using subexponential bounds on lower coefficients. -> sectorial uniform limits of families
- **step-prepared-contours** [claim](#stmt-path-prepared-contours) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-prepared-contours): Power sums by contour integrals, Newton identities, Cauchy division formula. -> Weierstrass preparation by fixed-contour operations
- **step-regular-roots** [claim](#stmt-path-roots) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-roots): Induction on degree, rescaling by largest coefficient size, splitting clusters of roots. -> algebraically closed complex scalars and real closed real scalars
- **step-detection** [claim](#stmt-path-detection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-detection): Submersive evaluation, measure zero of analytic zero sets, countable cover. -> detection of analytic identities
- **step-adjoin-reciprocal** [claim](#stmt-path-adjoin-p) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-adjoin-p): Disc and annular charts, Laurent decomposition, normalization, path substitution with a free constant. -> extended regular system with size bound
- **step-centers** [claim](#stmt-path-centers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-centers): Induction on the number of lengths: split the fastest block, adjoin the reciprocal, expand at grouped centers. -> regular system with centers and last-clock invariant
- **step-sign-elimination** [claim](#stmt-path-sign-elimination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-sign-elimination): Hermite trace form, signature computation and indicator polynomials, finite sign tests. -> finite coefficient sign tests for one ordinary variable
- **step-path-selection** [claim](#stmt-thm-path-selection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-sign-elimination-2): Choose a sign pattern on an ultrafilter set, eliminate ordinary variables one at a time, lift choices, place the path in a Hardy field. -> real analytic path with eventually monotone coordinates
- **step-variation-corollary** [claim](#stmt-path-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-path-variation): Apply path selection and integrate the logarithmic differential along the path. -> contradiction with the function tending to zero
- **step-disconnected-inverse** [claim](#stmt-ell-disconnected-inverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-disconnected-inverse): Explicit radial mode kernels on cylinders, parametrices on cores, Fredholm property, kernel tests and cokernel sources, Neumann series. -> uniform augmented inverse of disconnected operators
- **step-seam-inverse** [claim](#stmt-ell-seam-inverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-seam-inverse): Correct nondecaying incoming modes with explicit block trace matrices and perturbation. -> uniform inverse with finite seams
- **step-complex-inverse** [claim](#stmt-ell-complex-inverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-complex-inverse): Bent contours and strengthened source and seam norms with polynomial weights. -> inverse on quadratic complex length regions
- **step-gauge** [claim](#stmt-ell-gauge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-gauge): Contraction for the tension equation using the augmented vector isomorphism, then properness of the displacement map. -> gauged metric sequence
- **step-analytic-branch** [claim](#stmt-ell-analytic-family) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-analytic-family): Uniform contraction on complex domains, analytic dependence, contour independence by shape variation. -> single-valued holomorphic small family
- **step-comparison** [claim](#stmt-ell-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-comparison): Treat the Ricci error as a source, subtract, use the uniform inverse, integrate the first variation along a segment. -> estimates comparing the actual metric with the family
- **step-flat-tail** [claim](#stmt-ell-flat-tail) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-flat-tail): Polynomial primitives at resonances, full-cylinder solve at a noncritical weight. -> tail improvement for flat operators
- **step-prescribed-tails** [claim](#stmt-ell-prescribed-tails) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-prescribed-tails): Iterative extraction of tail powers, triangular recursion, cutoff solve with the fixed inverse. -> integer tails and finite prescribed growth
- **step-triangular** [claim](#stmt-ell-triangular) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-triangular): Successive solves of a triangular system with the same diagonal operator. -> auxiliary fields with uniform bounds
- **step-one-length** [claim](#stmt-ell-one-length) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-one-length): Induction on usable order, cancel bulk forcing, solve seam residuals with trace matrices, estimate the remainder. -> finite expansion in one length
- **step-finite-parts** [claim](#stmt-ell-finite-parts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-finite-parts): Finite-part normalization of integrals on opened ends and termwise integration. -> expansions of the scalar observables
- **step-all-orders** [claim](#stmt-ell-all-orders) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-all-orders): Iterate one-length expansions, bound coefficients, fix a common domain once for all partial disconnections. -> admissibility of the functional and the Ricci observable
- **step-differential** [claim](#stmt-ell-differential) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-differential): Dual-norm estimates for parameter derivatives and differentiation of seam data for length derivatives. -> gradient bound for the functional
- **step-tree-inequality** [claim](#stmt-thm-tree-inequality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ell-differential-2): Argue by contradiction on a subsequence and apply the variation corollary to the comparison family. -> the functional is little-oh of the Ricci error

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-descendant-scale**: Along a joined edge, the physical scale of the child vertex equals the parent scale times the factor e^{-2L_e}, with the root scale normalised.
- **inv-tree-little-oh**: For a fixed tree, along sequences with all joining lengths diverging and the weighted Ricci error tending to zero, the renormalized functional is little-oh of that error.
- **inv-sector-uniqueness**: A bounded holomorphic function on an admissible sector that decays faster than every exponential on the central subsector vanishes identically.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-slow-root-decay**: Apply the tree inequality to metrics whose root exterior end decays no faster than the borderline rate. -> The hypotheses behind integrability of the quadratic remainder and the first-variation pairing are lost, so the functional and its inequality are not controlled.
- **anti-trivial-quotient**: Use the result for trees in which a joined end has a trivial quotient group. -> The seam analysis uses the absence of invariant constant vectors, which needs a nontrivial freely acting group.
- **anti-uniform-exponent**: Read the little-oh estimate as a power-rate bound that is uniform over different trees. -> Constants and exponents belong to one fixed tree, so a uniform rate does not follow.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The results are analytic existence and asymptotic statements about families of metrics, holomorphic functions on complex length domains and little-oh estimates for a sequence. They are not finite procedures with a computable output: the proof argues by contradiction along a sequence and uses an ultrafilter and analytic continuation, so no finite reference implementation with an independent oracle can certify them.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an analytic inequality for degenerating geometric metrics. It specifies no program, interface or data format that could be implemented as software. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The results are asymptotic statements for sequences of four-dimensional Ricci-flat metrics. They give no discretised dynamics, time stepping or physical-simulation scheme. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The tree inequality and the path-selection theorem are existence and contradiction arguments. The only finite calculation, a sign test, is a proof device and not a tool specification. |
| algorithms | True | weak | adaptable | stated | Univariate sign elimination is a finite decision procedure. It builds a Hermite trace form for the polynomial product or its derivative, finds the signature by pivoting on a Schur complement, and counts sign types by indicator polynomials. The theorems around it (path selection, tree inequality) are non-constructive. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No container, index or representation with access guarantees is proposed. The regular system is an abstract algebraic invariant of germs on a domain base. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns arithmetic circuits, memory hierarchies or parallel kernels. The estimates are analytic and the sign test is symbolic. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no adversary model, hardness assumption or protocol. The decision procedure works over real closed fields and has no cryptographic content. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, training objective or generalisation bound occurs. The functional E and the Ricci norm J are geometric quantities on manifolds, not loss functions. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The argument bounds a functional by its gradient and then shows by contradiction that a ratio cannot persist along a path. It specifies no feasible set, objective or solver to run. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The tree in the paper is a geometric gluing pattern of Ricci-flat spaces. It carries no nodes, messages, failures or consensus structure. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The nearest uses are in geometric analysis and symbolic asymptotics. The gluing inverses (augmented by sources and tests) and path selection are proof tools with no executable specification. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-root-decay** (hypothesis): The functional and its first variation are controlled only when the root exterior end decays at a rate strictly above one. Blocked use: ['Applying the tree inequality to metrics whose root end decays at the borderline rate or more slowly', 'Assuming the boundary flux and scalar integral converge without that decay']
- **limit-nontrivial-quotient** (hypothesis): Each joined end must carry a nontrivial finite subgroup of O(4) acting freely on the three-sphere. Blocked use: ['Using the seam inverse for trees with a joined end having trivial quotient group']
- **limit-fixed-tree-sequential** (formal_scope): The little-oh estimate is sequential for one fixed tree and asserts no power exponent uniform over different trees. Blocked use: ['Quoting a uniform rate across families of trees', 'Using the estimate as a quantitative bound with explicit constants']
- **limit-diverging-lengths** (hypothesis): The inequality needs every joining length to diverge and the weighted Ricci error to tend to zero. Blocked use: ['Applying it at bounded neck lengths', 'Applying it when the Ricci error does not vanish in the limit']
- **limit-admissible-class** (hypothesis): Path selection applies only to functions satisfying the admissibility conditions, including common analyticity domains for all recursively extracted coefficients. Blocked use: ['Applying path selection to merely smooth or oscillatory asymptotics', 'Applying it to functions with only bounded holomorphy and no all-order expansion']
- **limit-nonconstructive-path** (nonconstructive): The path is produced by comparing sequence values through a nonprincipal ultrafilter and the tree inequality is proved by contradiction, so no explicit finite procedure or rate results. Blocked use: ['Treating the path construction as an algorithm', 'Extracting an effective rate from the contradiction argument']
- **limit-seam-inverse-lengths** (edge_case): The uniform seam inverse holds only for sufficiently large real lengths, and its complex continuation only on a quadratic region. Blocked use: ['Using the inverse bounds at small or moderate lengths', 'Using it outside the quadratic complex region']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove a sequential elliptic inequality for the renormalized Einstein–Hilbert functional on a fixed finite tree of four-dimensional Ricci-flat spaces. As the joining lengths diverge and the scale-neutral weighted Ricci error <i>M</i> tends to zero, the functional satisfies $`E=o(M)`$. The root end has decay exponent greater than one, and every joined quotient group is nontrivial. We also prove the multivariable path-selection theorem for asymptotic expansions used to obtain this estimate.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Ricci-flat metrics can degenerate by developing regions at several widely separated length scales. In four dimensions, an asymptotically locally Euclidean end of one region can join a neighborhood of an orbifold point in another. Repeating this configuration produces a finite rooted tree. The geometry on every fixed vertex region may converge smoothly while ratios of scales tend to zero. An estimate useful in this situation must control the whole joined metric, including the regions between its limiting pieces.

We study a renormalized Einstein–Hilbert functional on such metrics. The leading Euclidean divergence in the scalar-curvature density is subtracted at the exterior end of the root. The resulting quantity, denoted by $E$, has length degree two. Its differential is the integral pairing with the Einstein tensor $\tfrac12 Rg-\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}$. A fixed root with decay $g-\delta=O(r^{-q})$, $q>1$, makes the quadratic remainder and the first-variation pairing integrable. The individual scalar integral and boundary flux need not converge.

Renormalization of curvature energies on noncompact spaces has several established forms. Haslhofer’s renormalized Perelman functional treats relative energies near steady solitons (Haslhofer 2011) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Haslhofer2011](#ref-Haslhofer2011)]. Deruelle and Ozuch subtract the mass contribution in their analytic ALE functional and discuss explicitly the corresponding renormalized Hilbert–Einstein expression (Deruelle and Ozuch 2025, Definition 4.2, Remark 4.3, and Proposition 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[DeruelleOzuch2025](#ref-DeruelleOzuch2025)]. Their weighted Łojasiewicz inequality near a fixed Ricci-flat ALE background holds without an integrability hypothesis; integrability gives an improved exponent (Deruelle and Ozuch 2025, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[DeruelleOzuch2025](#ref-DeruelleOzuch2025)]. The functional $E$ here is the unminimized scalar-curvature-minus-flux expression, whereas their $\lambda_{\mathrm{ALE}}$ also minimizes over a potential. Our estimate concerns simultaneous degeneration of the joining scales in a fixed tree, outside one fixed weighted neighborhood of a smooth ALE metric.

The main theorem, stated with the geometric hypotheses in Section <a href="#sec:elliptic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elliptic">2</a>, gives a strict improvement over the direct first-variation estimate. Let $M$ control ten scaled derivatives of the dimensionless Ricci tensor, with positive exponential decay on joining half-ends and decay $r^{-q}$ at the root. For a fixed limiting tree and sequences with $M\to0$ and all joining lengths tending to infinity, Theorem <a href="#thm:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree-inequality">2</a> proves $E=o(M)$. The metrics converge to the specified vertex metrics on compact sets, and their end bounds are uniform through order twenty. Kernels and cokernels of the linearized equations are retained in the argument.

This is a sequential statement for one fixed tree. Joining lengths may diverge at unrelated rates, and the proof does not require the infinitesimal Ricci-flat deformations to integrate to a smooth moduli space. These features are relevant to four-dimensional Ricci-flow concentration, where the tree is obtained only after choosing a sequence of times. For a compact four-dimensional Ricci flow on a finite time interval with bounded scalar curvature, a curvature blow-up that converges smoothly to an ancient flow has a Ricci-flat ALE limit (Bamler and Zhang 2017, arXiv version, Corollary 1.9) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[BamlerZhangI](#ref-BamlerZhangI)]. Related distance-control and compactness results appear in (Bamler and Zhang 2019; Bamler 2018) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[BamlerZhangII](#ref-BamlerZhangII), [BamlerConvergence](#ref-BamlerConvergence)]. The present article proves the static estimate; its hypotheses are stated independently of a flow.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Why an analytic path enters the proof

On a fixed finite-dimensional analytic family, a gradient inequality relates a function to its differential near a critical set. The parameters here include neck lengths $L_e\to\infty$. Their boundary is outside an ordinary analytic coordinate neighborhood, and a change such as $t_e=e^{-L_e}$ need not make the family analytic at $t_e=0$. Asymptotic power-logarithm expansions can exist to every finite order without defining a convergent power series.

Our substitute is a path-selection statement for a precise class of holomorphic functions. In each length these functions have expansions in $e^{-dL}$ with polynomial coefficients in $L$, where the nonnegative exponent sets are locally finite. The coefficients admit the same expansions in the other lengths. Remainders are strictly better than the requested exponential order, and all recursively extracted coefficients are holomorphic on a common ordinary-parameter neighborhood. These hypotheses are defined in Definition <a href="#path:def-admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:def-admissible">3</a>.

Theorem <a href="#thm:path-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:path-selection">4</a> realizes finite sign conditions and finite prescribed limits along a real analytic path. Every actual length coordinate and every bounded ordinary coordinate is eventually monotone or constant. Therefore the path has finite variation in the weighted differential form $$\sum_\alpha |dz_\alpha|+\sum_e e^{-cL_e}|dL_e|,
 \qquad c>0.$$ This finite variation is the exact property used in the elliptic argument. The construction does not replace an all-order expansion by a convergent series.

Quasianalytic asymptotic algebras provide the ancestry of this approach. Speissegger (Speissegger 2018) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[SpeisseggerQA](#ref-SpeisseggerQA)] develops one-variable Ilyashenko algebras, and Galal, Kaiser, and Speissegger (Galal, Kaiser, and Speissegger 2020) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GalalKaiserSpeissegger](#ref-GalalKaiserSpeissegger)] construct algebras based on transserial asymptotic expansions. Their one-variable field and differentiation results provide precedents for the clock calculus used here. Our simultaneous length specialization, ordinary parameter normalization, and path selection are proved in Section <a href="#sec:path" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:path">3</a>; no multivariable selection theorem is imported from those works.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The elliptic reduction and its quantitative output

The analytic family is built in a gauge whose principal part is a componentwise scalar elliptic operator. The cancellation underlying this use of coordinates is part of the Ricci-coordinate method; see DeTurck and Kazdan (DeTurck and Kazdan 1981) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[DeTurckKazdan1981](#ref-DeTurckKazdan1981)] for its classical geometric setting. On each fixed vertex, finite core tests separate the kernel, while finitely many compactly supported sources represent the cokernel. On a joined edge, the two Cauchy matching conditions are supplemented by the value in the critical spherical channel. Explicit radial mode matrices then give an inverse whose bound is uniform as the lengths diverge.

This reduction has close predecessors in Einstein desingularization. Biquard constructs metrics modulo a finite-dimensional obstruction space in the Eguchi–Hanson gluing problem (Biquard 2013, arXiv version, Proposition 8.1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Biquard2011](#ref-Biquard2011)]. Ozuch develops uniform inverse estimates and nearby Einstein-modulo-obstructions families for desingularization trees, including ALE orbifolds (Ozuch 2022, arXiv version, Proposition 11 and Theorem 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Ozuch2022II](#ref-Ozuch2022II)]. The original development was also informed by Ozuch’s treatment of potentially nonintegrable Einstein deformations (Ozuch 2021) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Ozuch2021Integrability](#ref-Ozuch2021Integrability)]. This is methodological motivation, not an invocation of that paper’s results for the tree inequality proved here. Our proof retains that obstruction-space viewpoint. The additional interfaces needed here are continuation in complex length regions, recursive finite-order expansions with a common ordinary-parameter domain, and a differential estimate for the renormalized observable.

The ordinary parameters $z$ record these finite data. The length parameters are continued to quadratic complex regions. A uniform contraction there gives a small family $h_*(L,z)$ and, for a metric $g$ in the theorem, the comparison $$\|g-h_*\|\le CM,\qquad
 \|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(h_*)\|\le CM,\qquad
 |E(g)-E(h_*)|\le CM^2.$$ The norms and the gauge are specified in Section <a href="#sec:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:family">4</a>. This comparison is why the finite-dimensional family is sufficient even though the original metrics depend on infinitely many variables.

Set $F=E(h_*)$. Let $J$ be the nonnegative squared Ricci norm defined in Equation <a href="#ell:observables" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:observables">[ell:observables]</a>, with an additional weight on the root tail. The first variation and the shrinking physical volumes of descendant regions imply $$|dF|\le C\sqrt J
 \left(\sum_\alpha|dz_\alpha|+\sum_e e^{-cL_e}|dL_e|\right).$$ The remaining analytic obligation is to place $F$ and $J$ in the path-selection class. We establish this by opening one edge at a time. Solutions on an opened end have integer exponential tails. Finite incoming modes cancel the seam mismatch, and triangular linear auxiliary equations produce each additional asymptotic coefficient. Finite-part integration transfers the expansions to $F$ and $J$. Every order uses the same small nonlinear branch and the same basic inverse; coefficients with greater prescribed growth enter only linear solves. This preserves one ordinary-parameter domain through all orders.

If the tree inequality failed, the comparison would produce a sequence with $F\ne0$, $F\to0$, and $F^2\ge c_0J$ for some $c_0>0$. Path selection would preserve those conditions on a path of finite weighted variation. Dividing the differential estimate by $|F|$ would then give finite variation of $\log|F|$, contradicting $F\to0$. Corollary <a href="#path:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:variation">5</a> isolates this short final argument from the substantial analytic work needed to apply it.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization and conventions

Section <a href="#sec:elliptic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elliptic">2</a> defines the tree geometry, the Ricci error, and the functional and states the main theorem. Section <a href="#sec:path" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:path">3</a> proves path selection. Section <a href="#sec:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:family">4</a> constructs the inverses, gauge, analytic family, and comparison with the original sequence. Sections <a href="#ell:tail-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:tail-calculus">5</a> and <a href="#ell:length-expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:length-expansion">6</a> prove the end and length expansions. Section <a href="#ell:scalar-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:scalar-calculus">7</a> establishes the scalar expansion class and completes the theorem.

We use $\Delta=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nabla^2$. Metric and tensor components on a quotient end are equivariant Cartesian components on its Euclidean cover; all integrals are quotient integrals. Scaled derivative norms insert one local length factor per derivative. Tensor magnitudes are physical unless vertex units are explicitly specified. Constants may depend on the fixed tree, its charts, and a fixed finite differentiation order. Uniformity in diverging lengths or in an approximation index is asserted at the point where it is needed.

<a id="sec:elliptic"></a>
### Tree geometry and the renormalized functional

The result of this section is sequential. Its constants, its auxiliary analytic family, and its small exponents belong to one fixed limiting tree. No uniform exponent over different trees is asserted or needed.

<a id="ell:setup"></a>
#### The geometric data and the functional

<a id="ell:end-decay"></a>
Let $\mathcal T$ be a finite rooted tree of four-dimensional Ricci-flat spaces $(V_v,h_v)$. A vertex has one exterior end and one punctured end for each child. Every end has Cartesian coordinates on a Euclidean annulus modulo a finite subgroup of $O(4)$ acting freely on $S^3$. The group on a joined end is nontrivial. The two groups on an edge are identified; a fixed orthogonal identification is included in the charts. Put $\tau=\log r$ on an exterior end and $\tau=-\log r$ at a puncture. In these charts, in vertex units, $$h_v=\delta+O(e^{-\mu\tau})
 \label{ell:end-decay}$$ on joined ends, with derivatives at scale, for some $\mu>0$. On the unjoined exterior end of the root we require instead $h_v-\delta=O(r^{-q})$, where $q>1$. All remaining parts are smooth compact manifolds with the indicated boundary collars. Bounds through order $20$ suffice in the data below.

For an edge $e$ from $p$ to $c$, truncate both end coordinates at $\tau=L_e$ and identify the boundary collars by <a id="ell:scales"></a>$$x_p=e^{-2L_e}x_c,\qquad a_c=a_p e^{-2L_e},\qquad a_{\rm root}=1.
 \label{ell:scales}$$ Thus the metric tensor in vertex units is multiplied by $a_v^2$ in physical units. The physical radius $\rho=a_vr$ agrees in both charts, and $\tau_p+\tau_c=2L_e$. Gluing just the boundary faces, with their compatible smooth collars, gives the same manifold. Figure <a href="#fig:tree-scales" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:tree-scales">1</a> records how the two logarithmic coordinates meet at one physical radius and how edge lengths determine descendant scales.

<a id="eq:tree-ricci-error"></a>
Consider a sequence of metrics $g$ for which every $L_e\to\infty$, which converges to $h_v$ on each compact subset of every open vertex. Assume the same end estimates as in Equation <a href="#ell:end-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:end-decay">[ell:end-decay]</a> on each joining half, and the stated root estimate, with uniform finite derivative bounds. On a compact core put $\rho=a_v$. Suppose $M\to0$ and $$|\rho^2\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(g)|_j\le CM
 \begin{cases}
 e^{-\mu\tau},&\text{on joined half-ends},\\
 r^{-q},&\text{on the root exterior end},\\
 1,&\text{on vertex cores},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad j=10.
 \label{eq:tree-ricci-error}$$ Here the tensor magnitude is physical, and the subscript includes covariant derivatives multiplied by the corresponding powers of $\rho$. The metric bounds convert these to coordinate estimates. Decreasing $\mu$ or $q$, while retaining $\mu>0$ and $q>1$, is allowed.

<a id="eq:energy-functional"></a>
The subtraction below is the linear Euclidean boundary flux in the scalar-curvature density. Compare the mass-subtracted variational construction in (Deruelle and Ozuch 2025, Remark 4.3 and Proposition 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[DeruelleOzuch2025](#ref-DeruelleOzuch2025)]. We give its normalization and first variation directly for the present finite-tree geometry. Define, in physical root units, $$E(g)=\lim_{D\to\infty}\left\{
 \int_{r<D}R(g)\,d\mu_g-
 \int_{r=D}(\partial_jg_{ij}-\partial_ig_{jj})n_i\,dS_\delta
 \right\}.
 \label{eq:energy-functional}$$ All chart integrals are quotient integrals. The two terms inside the braces need not converge separately.

<a id="stmt-ell-functional"></a>
<div id="ell:functional" class="lemma">

<a id="ell:first-variation"></a>
**Lemma 1** (Renormalization and first variation). *The functional in Equation <a href="#eq:energy-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-functional">[eq:energy-functional]</a> is defined on the preceding metrics. For a variation with the same root decay, $$DE_g(\dot g)=\int\left\langle\tfrac12R(g)g-\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(g),\dot g\right\rangle_g
 \,d\mu_g.
 \label{ell:first-variation}$$ It is invariant under diffeomorphisms isotopic to the identity whose root displacement, including scaled derivatives throughout the isotopy, is $O(r^{1-q'})$ for some $q'>1$. A global length scaling by $s$, accompanied by a coordinate dilation at infinity restoring the leading tensor $\delta$, multiplies $E$ by $s^2$. Under Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a>, $|E(g)|\le CM$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ell-functional"><summary>Proof of stmt-ell-functional</summary>

*Proof.* Write $g=\delta+p$ on the root end. The scalar-curvature density is $$R(g)\sqrt{\det g}=
 \partial_i(\partial_jp_{ij}-\partial_ip_{jj})+
 O(|p||\partial^2p|+|\partial p|^2).$$ The last expression is $O(r^{-2q-2})$ and its radial integral is bounded by $C\int_{r_0}^\infty r^{1-2q}\,dr$. This proves convergence. The linear boundary term in the variation of scalar curvature cancels the variation of the subtracted integral. Every remaining boundary product is $O(D^{2-2q})$; it tends to zero. Integration by parts therefore gives Equation <a href="#ell:first-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:first-variation">[ell:first-variation]</a>. For a Lie derivative, the contracted Bianchi identity makes the interior divergence vanish. The remaining boundary term vanishes with the stated displacement decay. Integration along the isotopy gives invariance. Changing variables under a dilation in Equation <a href="#eq:energy-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-functional">[eq:energy-functional]</a> gives the scaling law.

Differentiate this law at $s=1$. Choose the compensating dilation to be the identity inside a fixed outer root region. Its metric variation equals $2g$ on the interior and decays like $r^{-q}$ on the root tail. Its pairing with Ricci on the root is integrable by $q>1$. On a punctured half the interior estimate costs $$CM a_p^2\int_{\tau_0}^{L_e}e^{-(2+\mu)\tau}\,d\tau\le CM a_p^2.$$ On a child exterior half the corresponding quantity is $$CM a_c^2\int_{\tau_0}^{L_e}e^{(2-\mu)\tau}\,d\tau
 \le CM a_p^2(1+L_e)e^{-\min(2+\mu,4)L_e},$$ with a harmless change of constant for the fixed lower endpoint. The finite sum of core contributions is bounded as well, since $a_v\le1$. Equation <a href="#ell:first-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:first-variation">[ell:first-variation]</a> now gives $2|E(g)|\le CM$. In particular $E=0$ when $\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-tree-inequality"></a>
<div id="thm:tree-inequality" class="theorem">

<a id="ell:little-oh"></a>
**Theorem 2** (Tree inequality). *Fix a finite rooted tree with the end identifications and order-$20$ metric bounds of Section <a href="#ell:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ell:setup">2.1</a>. Let $(g_j,L^{(j)},M_j)$ satisfy the compact vertex convergence, the same uniform half-end and root-end bounds, and Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a> through order $10$, with every $L_e^{(j)}\to\infty$ and $M_j\to0$. Then, in physical root units, $$E(g_j)=o(M_j).
 \label{ell:little-oh}$$ For an index with $M_j=0$, the assertion means $E(g_j)=0$. The little-oh is taken for this fixed tree; it does not assert a power exponent uniform over different trees.*

</div>

The proof constructs a finite-dimensional analytic family with complex length parameters. It does not assume that Einstein deformations are unobstructed, or that the given metrics or the coefficients of a fixed core metric are spatially analytic.

<figure id="fig:tree-scales">

<figcaption>A joined parent puncture and child exterior use opposite logarithmic coordinates. Their seam has physical radius $a_{p}e^{−L_{e}} = a_{c}e^{L_{e}}$ and $a_{c}/a_{p} = e^{−2L_{e}}$. The inset records a finite tree with independently diverging edge lengths; neither relative length ratios nor rates of divergence are fixed. The diagram is schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="sec:path"></a>
### Selection of paths at several exponential ends

The reduction used to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree-inequality">2</a> needs a path on which every original parameter has finite weighted variation. The parameters include several independently diverging neck lengths. Ordinary analytic curve selection does not directly apply at those ends. We give the precise asymptotic class and the selection argument used below. In particular, the argument includes the specialization of several lengths; one-variable quasianalyticity alone would not supply that step.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The class of functions and the selection statement

Put $$Q(A,c)=\{L\in\mathbb{C}:\Re L>A,\ |\Im L|<c(\Re L)^2\}.$$ An ordinary parameter is a variable in a fixed complex neighborhood of a finite real point. A real holomorphic function is one satisfying $P(\overline L,\overline z)=\overline{P(L,z)}$.

<a id="stmt-path-def-admissible"></a>
<div id="path:def-admissible" class="definition">

**Definition 3**. An admissible family in $m$ lengths consists of real holomorphic functions $P(L_1,\ldots,L_m,z)$, with the following properties. These properties are required for every recursively extracted coefficient as well as for the initial functions.

1.  The functions are bounded on a product of regions $Q(A,c)$, locally uniformly in the ordinary parameters.

2.  For each length there is an expansion <a id="path:eq-expansion"></a>$$\label{path:eq-expansion}
     P(L,z)\sim\sum_{d\in D_i}e^{-dL_i}
                 \sum_{k=0}^{k_d}L_i^k P_{dk}(L_{\widehat i},z).$$ Here $D_i\subset[0,\infty)$ is locally finite, each $k_d$ is finite, and $k_0=0$ if $0\in D_i$. For every cutoff $B$, subtracting the terms with $d\le B$ leaves a remainder bounded by $C_Be^{-(B+\epsilon_B)\Re L_i}$, for some $\epsilon_B>0$, on possibly smaller quadratic regions. Bounds are uniform on each fixed smaller ordinary-parameter polydisc.

3.  For every subset of expanded lengths, all its coefficient formulas, at all orders, are holomorphic on one common tail band $$\prod_{j\text{ unexpanded}}
     \{\Re L_j>A',\ |\Im L_j|<c'\},\qquad c'>0,$$ times one common open ordinary-parameter neighborhood. These domains may be shrunk once when a finite number of families are used together. The constants and thresholds in asymptotic *estimates* may depend on the order; the common domains of *analyticity* do not.

4.  If a subset of real parts has the form $\Re L_i=c_iU+o(U)$, where $c_i>0$, the corresponding iterated expansion can be grouped by weights $\sum_i c_id_i$. The grouped weights are locally finite, and each finite cutoff has a remainder of strictly better exponential order in $U$. The assertion holds on the complex neighborhoods in <span class="upright">(i)</span> and locally uniformly on the common ordinary-parameter neighborhood. It is enough to establish it by successive single-length expansions.

When $m=0$, admissible families are ordinary holomorphic germs.

</div>

The distinction in <span class="upright">(iii)</span> will be used when an expansion has infinitely many zero coefficients. It is not an assertion that all remainder estimates hold beyond a single order-independent threshold.

<a id="stmt-thm-path-selection"></a>
<div id="thm:path-selection" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Path selection). *Let $P_1,\ldots,P_s$ be an admissible finite family, and let a finite Boolean combination of conditions $P_j>0$, $P_j=0$, and $P_j<0$ hold along a sequence $$L_i^{(n)}\longrightarrow+\infty,\qquad z^{(n)}\longrightarrow z_0.$$ There is a real analytic map $u\mapsto(L(u),z(u))$, defined for all sufficiently large real $u$, with the same limits and satisfying those conditions on its tail. Each actual coordinate $L_i(u)$ and $z_a(u)$ is eventually monotone or constant. Given any additional finite admissible list, its values on the path can also be required to be eventually monotone or constant. Finite prescribed limits of functions can be imposed at the same time.*

</div>

A path germ here means analyticity at every sufficiently large finite $u$; analyticity at the limiting endpoint is not asserted. To impose $P\to\ell$, introduce an ordinary variable $v\to\ell$ and impose $P-v=0$. If there are no length variables, add an unused length. This also provides positive infinitesimals for strict sign conditions at an ordinary limiting point.

<a id="stmt-path-variation"></a>
<div id="path:variation" class="corollary">

**Corollary 5** (The variation consequence). *Suppose admissible functions $F,J$, with $J\ge0$, satisfy $$|dF|\le C\sqrt J\left(\sum_a|dz_a|
                  +\sum_i e^{-cL_i}|dL_i|\right),\qquad c>0,$$ on real parameter domains. There cannot be a sequence with $L_i\to\infty$, an interior ordinary-parameter limit, and $F\ne0$, $F^2\ge c_0J$, $F\to0$, where $c_0>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-path-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-path-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#thm:path-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:path-selection">4</a> realizes all these conditions on one path with monotone actual coordinates. Along it, $$|d\log|F||\le\frac C{\sqrt{c_0}}
       \left(\sum_a|dz_a|+\sum_i e^{-cL_i}|dL_i|\right).$$ Each $z_a$ has finite total variation because it is monotone with a finite limit. Each length tends monotonically to infinity on its tail, and $$\int_{u_0}^\infty e^{-cL_i(u)}|L_i'(u)|\,du
       =c^{-1}e^{-cL_i(u_0)}<\infty.$$ Thus $\log|F|$ has finite total variation and a finite limit, whereas $F\to0$ forces $\log|F|\to-\infty$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

The remaining subsections prove Theorem <a href="#thm:path-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:path-selection">4</a>. The geometric construction resumes in Section <a href="#sec:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:family">4</a> and uses Definition <a href="#path:def-admissible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:def-admissible">3</a> and Corollary <a href="#path:variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="path:variation">5</a> as its analytic interface.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Sectors, uniqueness, and clocks

<a id="stmt-path-def-sector"></a>
<div id="path:def-sector" class="definition">

**Definition 6**. An admissible angular loss is a positive continuous function $\omega(r)$, for large $r$, such that $$\omega(r)\ge(\log r)^{-2},\qquad
 \sum_{n\ge n_0}\sup_{2^n<r<2^{n+2}}\omega(r)<\infty.$$ Its sector is the open set $S_\omega=\{z:|z|>R,\ |\arg z|<\pi/2-\omega(|z|)\}$.
